CHAPTER 3: RAVENLOFT ADVENTURES Alanik Ray and Arthur Sedgwick investigate the occult art collection of the Marquis Stezen dPolarno @ Sean A, Murray Drafting frightful encounters, adventures, and campaigns requires deliberate choices that cultivate an atmosphere of dread. This chapter explores how you, the DM, can use the Domains of Dread to create suspense during play and craft terrifying adventures. CAMPAIGNS IN THE MISTS The surreal natures of the Domains of Dread, their Darklords, the Mists, and the Dark Powers serve as tools in your frightful adventures. None of them are too important to use, too mysterious to reveal, or too fragile to pit against characters. This section explores how to use the most infamous features of the Domains of Dread to create frightful campaigns. Before preparing horror adventures, review the "Ensuring Fun for All" section of the Dungeon Master's Guide. CREATING A HORROR CAMPAIGN When planning campaigns in Ravenloft, consider the guidance in chapter 5 of the Dungeon Master's Guide, along with the following topics. Record the answers to the questions in this section to inspire your campaign. CAMPAIGN CHARACTERS Characters exploring the Mists are the protagonists of horror stories. Chapter 1 provides advice for players creating such characters, but you should also consider the following questions:

Page 197

Character Origins. Do the characters originate from the Domains of Dread, or were they abducted from somewhere else? Context for Surreality. Do the characters realize something is strange about the Domains of Dread, or is the Land of the Mists all they've ever known? Scale of Experience. Do the characters know about a single domain—perhaps one they all call home—or do they have firsthand experience with multiple domains or other settings? Characters’ Significance. Why are the characters special? Why did the Dark Powers, a Darklord, or something else take note of them? Consistency of Experience. Do the answers to these questions apply to alll of the characters? CAMPAIGN THEMES Explore the types of horror adventures you're interested in by considering the following questions: Horror Inspirations. What horror stories or media inspire your campaign? Horror Icons. Is there a classic monster or villain that inspires the foes in your campaign? Horror Genres. What genres of horror—detailed in chapter 4 or elsewhere—does your campaign focus on? Domains of Dread. Which Domains of Dread or Darklords do you want to focus on? Or do you want to create a new Domain of Dread and Darklord following the methods in chapter 47 SETTING MYSTERIES The Domains of Dread function in unique ways, but if and how that matters is up to you. "Ravenloft Setting Models" later in this chapter details ways to shape the setting to suit your adventures. Review that section and the following questions as you consider how the nature of the Domains of Dread might shape your adventures: Escape. Do you want characters to pursue escape from the Domains of Dread? Do they want to leave even if a domain has been their home? Islands or Regions. Does your campaign focus on a single domain, or are there multiple domains you want to explore? Metaphysical Scale, Do you want your campaign to explore the unusual nature of the Domains of Dread, or is that nature broadly accepted reality? Stories and Secrets. Are tales of other domains or settings treated as facts, lore known only to a few, legends, or nonsense? Is that true for all places beyond a domain's borders? Traveling the Mists. Do people travel between domains? Is travel limited to wanderers such as Vistani, or are tools such as Mist talismans (see the introduction) common enough to allow for broader travel and trade? Witnesses to the Surreal. How do domain residents react to supernatural events? Are they shocked, do they treat them as commonplace, or do they rationalize (or deny) abject horrors? The answers to these questions are the ones that inspire you and your players. DARKLORD CONFLICTS Each Domain of Dread is a reflection of and a prison for a Darklord. The tales and torments of those Darklords are fundamental to adventures within their borders. Consider the following questions when developing your campaign: Darklord Plots. Will your campaign involve the history, torment, and potential defeat of a Darklord? Or would you rather keep a Darklord in the background and focus on other stories? Darklord Downfalls. Can a Darklord be defeated, or are they an enduring and recurring threat? What happens when a Darklord dies? Darklord Defiance. What does it mean to best a Darklord? Does it mean slaying them, forcing them to open their domain's borders, undermining their plans, or something else? Darklord Relationships. Does the Darklord want to destroy, conscript, or use the characters? Does the Darklord appear in person or communicate via proxies? Darklord Rivalries. Does your campaign feature interaction between multiple Darklords? If so, how do they know about each other? What do they have to gain from their conflicts? The "Using Darklords" section later in this chapter further explores ways to implement Darklords in your campaign. STARTING YOUR CAMPAIGN Start your campaign in a way that establishes an ominous tone or highlights the nature of the Domains of Dread. Consider the following questions: Collective Misfortune. Do the characters gather to mourn the death of a loved one? Does fate bring them together as the survivors of a disaster, such as a shipwreck or massacre? Darklord's Summons. When a Darklord calls, the beckoned answer. Do the characters meet after being hired by a Darklord to perform some task or by attending a social event that turns ominous?

Page 198

Present Terror. Do the characters meet amid terrifying circumstances, such as being prisoners or experiment subjects? Shared Mystery. Do the characters gather due to a shared mystery? Are they led to the same place by an enigmatic message, or do they start cooperating as they flee being accused of murder? Strangers in the Mists. Are the characters united by the Mists? Might they be traveling to other destinations when the Mists unite then? During character creation, you can work with players to come up with reasons why the characters might be in a grim or unusual situation. A horrific origin might also unite the party. Players might be interested in their characters sharing a species or a Dark Gift from chapter 1. A group composed entirely of Reborn or of characters who have the Aberrant Anatomy feat can provide ample inspiration for frightful adventures. CAMPAIGN CONSIDERATIONS Think about how you might reinforce a frightful mood over the course of your campaign. Consider the following questions about topics likely to occur during a series of adventures: Allies and Sanctuaries. Is it ever appropriate to harm characters’ allies or destroy places they consider safe? Is doing so worth making players fee! like they've lost something they've invested in? See "Tragic Limits’ in the Dungeon Master's Guide for advice on handling these topics. Altered States, How might characters change during a campaign? Do you want to encourage players to develop character changes resulting from their experiences~such as scars, phobias, or Dark Gifts? Character Secrets, What sorts of secrets might a character have, and are those boons to the game or potential disruptions? You can use the advice in the Dungeon Master's Guide on evil characters to incorporate characters with secrets into your game. Death, What happens when a character dies? How might a character brought back from death be changed? Fear. How do you expect characters to respond to frightful situations? Is fear reflected only by the Frightened condition, or would you like players to roleplay shock and revulsion? Hope. How will you give characters hope and ensure they don't feel like the campaign is pointless? What rays of light perforate the darkness? New Characters. If a character dies and isr't raised, how will you add a player's new character to the game? Do the Mists reveal a new hero, or might someone in a domain be inspired to act by another's sacrifice? When thinking about the answers to these questions, avoid changing the rules of the game—these should remain the stable, agreed-on underpinnings of the game. ENDING YOUR CAMPAIGN Horror stories often don't end well, but yours might be an exception, Have an idea of what makes a satisfying end for your horror campaign. Frame your thoughts on this by considering these questions: Escape. Is the goal of your campaign for the characters to escape a domain or all of the Domains of Dread? Can they achieve this through the defeat of a Darklord, the will of the Dark Powers, or some other magical method? Memorial. If a character dies, can they still contribute to a campaign's culmination? Can an accidental death become a meaningful sacrifice? Is there important work only a spirit can do? Survival. Do you expect any or all of the characters to survive the campaign? Will the players be satisfied if their characters’ stories end in defeat, transformation, or willing sacrifice? Worthy Cause. What's the goal the characters might risk everything to achieve? Is this protecting a person or place, or is it keeping something out of a Darklord’s hands? Is there a status quo that's vital to restore or a wrong that needs to be undone? Ensure you and the players agree on what makes a fulfilling end to a campaign. Don't work toward a climax demanding a dramatic sacrifice that no one's interested in making.

Page 199

# Rowan Holloway FURTHER DEVELOPMENT Review your answers to the questions from this section to see what patterns emerge and what answers surprised or excited you. Let those answers develop into ideas for unique horror adventures and arcs for your own campaigns. Next, review the following sections in this chapter. These explore ways to employ elements of the Domains of Dread in your horror adventures and outline whole frightful campaign arcs. UsING DOMAINS OF DREAD Domains of Dread are backdrops to horror tales and the torments of their Darklord. These domains exist to confound and terrify those within. Everything in a Domain of Dread serves frightful ends, and if something seems like it doesn't, you just haven't revealed how yet, While creating horror adventures, consider the following facts about domains. RAVENLOFT SETTING MODELS What domains compose the Ravenloft setting is up to you. The Domains of Dread have no definitive map or proscribed orientation for how they relate to one another. This allows you to shape the setting however best serves your adventures. Consider the following setting models for how you might use the Domains of Dread or use your favorite elements from multiple domains. HAUNTED HousE DOMAIN In this model, a single domain functions like a haunted house. Characters enter, terrifying forces trap them, and the party struggles to escape, This model features in the adventures /6: Ravenloft (1983) and its descendant Curse of Strahd (2016). This setting model excels if you want to. + Explore the tale of a single domain or Darklord,

Page 200

+ Highlight a narrow band of horror genres. * Avoid interacting with multiple domains or traveling the Mists. + Make a Darklord your adventures’ focal villain. ISLANDS OF TERROR In this model, the Domains of Dread are isolated realms that characters might reach by braving the Mists. Domains’ inhabitants might be aware of each other, but travel is uncommon and dangerous. Characters might be inhabitants of a domain or wayward souls from other realms, and events might lead them through the Mists to other nightmarish lands. This model is broadly used in this book. This setting model excels if you want to... + Highlight the mystery and surreal nature of the Domains of Dread. + Feature tours of multiple domains while controlling where the Mists lead. + Explore stories focused on the nature of the Mists and escaping the Domains of Dread, + Make Darklords part of a cast of villains. CLUSTERS AND CORES In this model, the Domains of Dread are similar to countries on globe. The Mists mark the edges of the world, rise when Darklords close their domains’ borders, and appear as they please, but otherwise domains are separated by geographical and political borders. Domain inhabitants know about one another, travel, and trade, while Darklords are powerful figures that might have political or warlike aspirations, Whether you create a cluster out of a small group of your favorite domains or meld many (or all) the domains into a continent-sized core is up to you. This is the model largely used in earlier explorations of the setting, including Ravenloft: Realm of Terror (1990) and Domains of Dread (1997). This setting model excels if you want to... + Encourage characters to roam between domains. + Highlight villains who are infamous in multiple domains or who must be pursued (or evaded). + Explore large-scale plots, such as wars between or the destruction of the Domains of Dread + Obscure Darklords, making them figures that might not be widely known, Gortnic EartH The similarities between Strahd von Zarovich and Dracula, between Viktra Mordenheim and Victor Frankenstein, and between other Ravenloft villains and icons of horror fiction and film are intentional. Because many of Ravenloft's villains and domains have real-world roots, elements from Ravenloft are readily portable into frightful adventures set on a version of Earth steeped in magic and gothic horror, While the details of such historic horror are beyond the scope of this book, consider using the tools herein to pit characters against menaces from your favorite horror tales. This model is explored in Ravenloft: Masque of the Red Death and Other Tales (1994). DoMAIN DETAILS To reinforce the types of horror your adventures explore and give domains their own malicious personalities, consider these setting elements.

Page 201

Those who enter the Mists... f Sylvain Sar DARKLORD REFLECTIONS Every domain can be viewed through the lens of its Darklord, with every part of the domain reflecting some facet of the Darklord’s personality, ambition, or torment. You might think about adventures in a domain as explorations of a Darklord's identity. For example, Barovia's gothic horror themes include deception, oppression, and predation, all of which are aspects of the domain's Darklord, ‘Strahd von Zarovich. While the village of Vallaki leans toward themes of deception, the village of Barovia, in the shadow of Castle Ravenloft, is steeped in oppression. The domain's deadly wildernesses and beasts reinforce themes of predation. When a domain's terrifying forces reflect aspects of its Darklord, that villain becomes a terrifying culmination of the evil in the land and the personification of the domain's terrors. FAMILIAR GROUND Every Domain of Dread takes inspiration from stories within its related genres. Embrace this. Including tropes from your favorite horror stories reinforces expectations about the domain and provides a shorthand to help players understand a location. The Domains of Dread are intentionally filled with gothic castles, haunted manors, and beleaguered villages to hint at the types of characters and adventures you'll find within. Don't worry about recreating familiar stories, as the unpredictable actions of the players’ characters will create a unique adventure. Twisted Tropes. Wildly deviating from tropes can create unique locations, but doing so requires more explanation to help players understand the setting. Consider instead featuring familiar horror settings with a twist to make them more appropriate to D&D or to enhance their terror. For example, characters escaping an alien laboratory in Bluetspur might be stalked by an elusive displacer beast, or the ghosts haunting a house in Mordent might be desperately trying to keep something worse sealed in the basement, Minor twists help setting elements feel distinct and work better in your game.

Page 202

ISOLATION Isolation can take multiple forms in a domain. Character Isolation. \n the Domains of Dread, the characters are often the land's only opposition to a domain's evil and their own only hope for survival, While characters might find allies or sanctuaries in a domain, the characters must look to themselves for solutions. Escape is rarely an option. Physical isolation. Each domain is a complete setting that the Mists might isolate from all other lands. Locations within a domain are often isolated by dark, monster-haunted wildernesses. Even settlements and sanctuaries might have their own grim secrets. might discover nightmares worse than they escaped # Sylvain Sarrailh THE LocALs The inhabitants of domains endure or ignore the horrors around them. Whether these individuals are oppressed people or soulless pawns of the Dark Powers is up to you, but regardless, they don't effectively resist the horror within their homes. Characters should feel like they're among the only ones who recognize a domain's evil. Jncongruous Behavior. Consider treating a domain's inhabitants as part of the strangeness of the setting. Such NPCs might willfully ignore proof of the domain's horrors or passively behave in ways that reinforce a Darklord's will. In more surreal cases, NPCs might forget the characters or even reappear after dying. NIGHTMARE NATURE To any question regarding the natural world within a domain, the scariest answer is likely true. Use details of a domain to create a beat of small, unsettling moments to support the percussion of unignorable terrors in your adventures. If you highlight the strange nature of a domain often, the land feels surreal and haunted. If you do so more rarely, the domain feels more realistic. You might even change the pace of how often you

Page 203

reinforce the strange nature of a domain, perhaps doing so more the farther characters are from civilization or safety. For examples of frightful details you might highlight in a domain, roll on or choose a result from the Dread Details table. Dread Details 148 In a Quiet Moment, a Character Witnesses... 1 Bestial eyes glaring from the shadows. Clouds briefly take on frightful shapes. ‘A door unlatch and open on its own A farm animal or pet curse under its breath Flowers turn black, then wither. A shadow or reflection move on its own. A spectral orb drift through a wall or door. eoNaanron A tree branch move like a grasping claw. You can customize these suggestions to reinforce the themes of a domain or its Darklord, For example, in Falkovnia, a statue might momentarily resemble a rotting zombie, while in Har'Akir, Darklord Ankhtepot's visage might appear in a sandstorm. Strive to make such elements feel like moments of strangeness—harmless oddities that a character can dismiss as thelr eyes deceiving them, rather than suggestions of an immediate threat. UsING DARKLORDS Second only to the characters, Darklords are the most important inhabitants of the Domains of Dread. This section provides details and options for using Darklords in horror campaigns. DARKLORDS IN YOUR ADVENTURES Most Darklords are important, recurring villains. Take time to understand these characters’ origins and develop details that you can consistently express as characters repeatedly encounter them, their agents, and evidence of their deeds. The Dungeon Master's Guide provides detalls on developing villains as well as an NPC Tracker sheet. Use the guidance there and in the following sections to develop Darklords into infamous villains your players will be excited to learn about and oppose. ROLEPLAYING A DARKLORD Most Darklords are irredeemably evil villains, obsessed with their own drives and desires. Many delude themselves into believing they're heroes or beings beyond morality. When preparing to roleplay a Darklord, review the Darklord's origin story and consider what traits are core to the Darklord. Aim to roleplay the Darklord in a way that makes sense with the villain's origins and that makes you excited to play them as a recurring character. Then consider the following questions: + What do you like most about the Darklord and want to highlight? + What are the Darklord's three most positive traits? + What are the Darklord's three most negative traits? + Why do others fear the Darklord? + What makes the Darklord effective in their role? + Does the Darklord think they're a hero or better than others? Why? * Does the Darklord always lie or never lie? + Howis the Darklord a reflection of one or more of the adventurers? + What does the Darklord want more than anything? + When would the Darklord be gracious or heroic? Record your answers to these questions, then create a short list of characteristics you want to convey to the players over encounters with the Darklord. DARKLORD GOALS In the adventure Curse of Strahd, the Darklord’s goals are stated as follows:

Page 204

+ Transform and possess a victim + Torment and slay a rival + Test or toy with the characters These goals guide Strahd's interactions with the characters through an entire campaign, allowing for the villain’s consistent representation. Recurring Darklords (or other prominent villains) should have similar, simple-to-understand goals. While you might know the finer details of a Darklord's motivations, players engage with villains only a limited number of times—likely with significant amounts of time between interactions. Choose one to three objectives for a Darklord that you can reinforce during character interactions or that you can hint at with other evidence. This helps the players understand what the Darklord wants and how to act against them. Use the goals presented above and on the list below to inspire your Darklord's objectives: Change society Enforce order Recover a lost treasure Claim a new role Escape their prison Relieve their boredom Continue a tradition Find a missing person Seek a revelation Correct a tragic event Maintain a lie Suppress a secret Defend the status quo Prevent the inevitable DARKLORD'S TALE A Darklord's villainy informs their goals and the torments they suffer. As characters adventure in a Darklord's domain, they learn about the Darklord, hear reports of the Darklord’s infamy, and eventually discover secrets to frustrate or undermine the villain, Consider these ways of revealing details about a Darklord: Rumors and Legends. Domain inhabitants typically know of a Darklord or the results of the villains evil. They can provide consistent reports of the Darklord's depredations, what little they know of the villain’s origins, and examples of how their lives are affected by the Darklord's evil. Evidence. Show the characters examples of the Darklord’s evil, the suffering of the villain’s victims, the crimes of the Darklord’s servants, and how ‘the domain is scarred by the Darklord’s wickedness. Lies, The Darklord or their servants provide flattering but false details of the villain's origins, personality, or objectives. Within such lies are truths commoners don't know. Primary Sources, The truth of a Darklord's past might come from the villain’s family, longtime servants or rivals, or historic records~such as journals, correspondence, or neglected artwork. Magical sources, such as mirrors that reflect the past or methods to enter the Darklord’s memories, might also reveal secrets. Such truths might be hidden, but the Dark Powers ensure they survive to torment the Darklord.

Page 205

The Tome of Strahd recounts the tale of Strahd von Zarovich, Other Darklords might keep similar records Jane Katsubo DarRKLorRD's MENACE Not every Darklord is a threat in combat to every party, but a Darklord's menace isn't rooted in their stat block. Rather, it's built over adventures where evidence of the Darklord's wickedness is established and reinforced, Consider the following questions to develop a Darklord's reputation, adding them as features of adventures: Demonstrations. When the characters offend the Darklord, how does the villain express displeasure? Do they take it out on the domain's inhabitants? Do they dub the characters criminals or heretics and execute innocent people who helped the characters? Law and Superstition. What do the domain's inhabitants do or refuse to do out of fear of the Darklord or the situation the Darklord has created? Have these habits become local traditions? Servants. What serves the Darklord? Are they soldiers and servants? Are they monsters or strangely behaving animals? Is it the weather or environment itself? How do such servants make it clear they're extensions of the Darklord's will? Signature Evidence. How does the Darklord prey or experiment on the domain's inhabitants and what evidence does that leave? If the Darklord executes or sacrifices foes, what is their signature form of slaughter? Strangers. Do domain inhabitants believe strangers are agents of the Darklord or likely to attract the Darklord's attention? Do they avoid strangers or spy on them for the Darklord? Titles. What is the Darklord's title? Is it a royal or religious title, or is it an epithet the domain's inhabitants call them?

Page 206

MEETING A DARKLORD Your players’ first encounter with a Darklord should be memorable. Prior to this encounter, the characters might have heard rumors of the Darklord and witnessed their depravity. Reinforce this by planning how the characters will meet the Darklord and what the villain will be doing, Consider whether the Darklord is.. + Attempting to hire the characters. + Commanding the Darklord's servants. + Committing a signature crime. + Engaged in an experiment. + Having a decadent or horrible meal. + Impatient with the characters’ interruption Pretending to be heroic. Trying to lure a victim. Pay attention to the Darklord's first words to the characters, Chapter 2 presents greetings along with many Darklords' portraits, Take inspiration from these greetings, and have the Darklord's first words reflect their self-obsessed personalities and how they perceive strangers. In future meetings, the Darklord should behave consistently. You might develop a signature way of speaking, turns of phrase, or an in-character voice for a Darklord, but keep these simple so the villain doesn't feel like a caricature or comedic figure. DARKLORD ALLIES Determine who serves the Darklord, A Darklord's servants should feel like facets of the Darklord's nature or goals. Characters should overcome these foes during adventures wherein they learn more about the Darklord, Defeating lesser villains gives characters a sense of progress while maintaining a Darklord's menace. Make a Darklord's allies exciting, but don't lose sight of the Darklord as the most important villain. When developing allies for a Darklord, consider the following types of allies: Advisers, Advisers grant the villain insights or control they wouldn't otherwise possess. Copies. Some servants are lesser versions of the Darklord. They do the same things as the Darklord to a lesser extent, or they're a weaker version of the same creature as the Darklord. Creations. A Darklord might be served by their creations, such as Construct or Undead servants. A relic or ritual might maintain the Darklord’s control of such creatures, Devotees. Devotees of a Darklord follow them without question, They might be whole organizations of soldiers or worshippers. Grudging Allies, Some servants obey because they must. They might serve the Darklord to keep loved ones safe or out of a sense of duty. Incidental Allies. Some allies don't directly serve the Darklord, but they and the Darklord both gain benefits from their partnership. A monster the Darklord keeps fed with sacrifices would be such an ally. Majordomo. A majordomo is likely the Darklord’s most powerful servant—the one who knows the most about them and, potentially, the one most likely to betray the Darklord. Mini Darklords. Some servants control their own portions of a domain and embody the Darklord's will in that region. Individual allies can occupy multiple roles.

Page 207

The Holy Symbol of Ravenkind is one example of a relic used to defeat a Darklord # Jane Katsubo TING A DARKLORD Slaying a villain isn't in itself a heroic goal, but the death of a villain generally means an end to its evil and its ability to affect others. Such isn't true of Darklords. Typically, when a Darklord is defeated in combat, their domain doesnt change, and fear of the Darklord doesnit fade. Moreover, the Darklord returns after a short time. While a Darklord is sidelined, their domain-affecting powers end, their servants go without orders, and the domains’ borders open. Characters might enter the Mists and find their threats reduced, but this doesn't mean the Mists allow travel to other lands. ‘Thwarting a Darklord and escaping their clutches ultimately means delivering the villain a dramatic defeat—one that sends them into such a state of rage, desolation, or uncertainty that their grip on their domain is undermined. Such a defeat might involve a climactic battle following a series of heroic accomplishments. If you plan a climactic confrontation with a Darklord—after which the characters escape the Darklord's domain— determine how the characters must subvert the villain. These requirements should develop from the Darklord's origins and as counters to the goals you've developed for the villain (see "Darklord Goals’ earlier in this chapter). Take inspiration for additional goals for the characters from the suggestions here: Destruction. Destroy what the Darklord draws their power from, what they've been working to create, or their sanctuary. Escape. Help a victim or captive of the Darklord escape the domain, or ensure they can't be part of the Darklord's plans. Isolation. Disrupt those who empower the Darklord—like their soldiers, cultists, creations, or other allies—forcing the Darklord to spend years to make more. Relic, Recover a symbol, icon, or weapon that is anathema to the Darklord. Rivals. Empower foes of the Darklord, resurrect storied heroes, restore bastions of opposition, or reawaken sites of good magic within the domain

Page 208

‘Truth. Reveal the Darklord as a fraud or pretender to their position. Characters should achieve these goals over the course of multiple adventures, culminating in a climactic encounter where the Darklord discovers the full extent of the characters’ successes. UNEXPECTED ENDS It's fine if characters defeat a Darklord in combat before you intend. A Darklord loses control only after extraordinary events during an adventure's climax. Until then, any defeat is an inconvenience or was part of a Darklord's plans—in any case, it's an opportunity for adventures, Don't rob characters of the accomplishment of a victory, but don't let them think they've won for long. Use the suggestions below to reinforce a Darklord’s threat even after an unexpected defeat. Familiar Death, This isn't Darklord's first death. As they die, a Darklord might be amused, blasé, cocky, or fascinated, but not angry or fearful. As it departs, a Darklord might congratulate the characters or narrate their own end. Before dying, the Darklord should make it clear that the characters now have the Darklord's full attention. ‘No Remains, Darklords don't usually leave corpses. When a Darklord is defeated, the Mists claim their body, or the corpse dissolves in an unsettling or thematically appropriate effect. Deadly in Defeat. With the Darklord's defeat, what contingencies take effect? Perhaps the Darklord left a letter or a gift to be delivered on the event of their death, Maybe their servants have orders about what to do on the event of the Darklord's disappearance—like commencing an attack or making a sacrifice, Perhaps threats the Darklord was restraining~such as hidden monsters or volatile servants~run wild, making the domain's inhabitants desire the Darklord's return. In any case, use a Darklord's defeat to demonstrate that the villain is just the face of a much larger, evil system. The Cat's Away. Use a Darklord's defeat to allow characters to have an adventure they couldn't have had otherwise. Perhaps new allies feel emboldened to share information or the guardians of the Darklord's lair are distracted. Use the Darklord’s absence as a chance to reveal more about the villain and provide the characters with the tools they need to defeat the Darklord once and for all. The Darklord's Shadows. The Darklord might be temporarily gone, but their evil isn't. During the Darklord’s absence, consider ways the domain might focus more on the characters. Perhaps everyone in the domain whispers about them, wolves are curious about them, zombies flee from them, or the Mists seem to follow them. Cultivate the idea that the Darklord is still watching them or that the characters have violated a taboo. The Darklord's Return, Determine how the Darklord returns and what they do once they're back. Does the Darklord seek revenge against the party? Is the Darklord more curious about—or obsessed with—the characters (see "Darklord Patrons” below)? Does the Darklord invite the characters to their home, devise a trap for them, or seek to involve them in some broader plot? Or was the Darklord's defeat truly inconsequential, with the characters learning of the villain's return only well afterward? DARKLORD PATRONS Most Darklords have lost track of how long they've been prisoners of their domains. Countless times they've failed to achieve their goals, and countless times the Dark Powers have tempted them with hope and opportunity again. Darklords seek new factors that might finally allow them to achieve their desires. And few factors hold more potential to further Darklords' plots~or simply distract them for a time—than the characters. Darklords might become fascinated by characters, viewing them as potential allies, protégés, tools, or challenges to overcome. In such cases, the Darklord might patronize or test the characters, sending them on quests throughout the Darklord’s domain or into the domains of rival Darklords. In return for the characters’ aid, the Darklord might offer the characters escape, secrets, or treasures, or the villain might promise not to harm innocents or the characters’ allies. Darklords might serve as typical quest givers, but they tend to be more possessive of their allies. At your discretion, a Darklord's magic might grant all the characters a Dark Gift from those presented in chapter 1, Alternatively, the Darklord might grant characters a sinister supernatural gift. DARKLORD GIFTS Darklords might bestow a supernatural gift on characters they've allied with (see "Supernatural Gifts’ in the Dungeon Master's Guide for details). These sinister Blessings might be a symbol of a promise made between the Darklord and the recipient, or they might be a reward for the characters’ service. A character must accept a Darklord’s Blessing to receive it. The Blessing can be removed by the Remove Curse spell, or the Darklord might revoke it anytime. The following Blessings are examples of those a Darklord might bestow upon a character. Customize them to create gifts appropriate to specific Darklords. Mark OF BEASTS Supernatural Gift (Blessing) On granting this Blessing, the Darklord chooses a Beast associated with themself. While you are in the same domain as the Darklord, Beasts of that kind are Friendly toward you. Additionally, you can use this Blessing to cast Speak with Animals to communicate with this kind of Beast only. Once you cast this spell in this way, you cant cast it again until you finish a Long Rest.

Page 209

Mark OF THE DREAD LoRD ‘Supernatural Gift (Blessing) While in the same domain as the Darklord who gave you this Blessing, you can cast Fear. Once you cast this spell in this way, you can't cast it again until you finish a Long Rest. So long as you possess this Blessing, your face is veiled by shadows. MARK OF OBSESSION ‘Supernatural Gift (Blessing) Immediately after you take damage from a creature you can see within 10 feet of yourself, you can take a Reaction to force the creature to make a DC 14 Wisdom saving throw. On a failed save, the creature has the Frightened condition until the end of your next turn. Once you use this Blessing, you can't use it again until you finish a Long Rest. While you have this Blessing, the Darklord who gave it to you can contact you once every 24 hours. This might take the form of a magical message, such as a Sending spell, or a short letter delivered by mysterious means. MARK OF SACRIFICE Supernatural Gift (Blessing) You can use this Blessing to cast Protection from Evil and Good. Once you cast this spell in this way, you can't cast it again until you finish a Long Rest. If this Blessing is removed, the Darklord who gave it to you knows your whereabouts, and you have Disadvantage on Wisdom saving throws for 24 hours ESCAPING RAVENLOFT The Domains of Dread are as escapable as you please. The Mists might claim characters for a single adventure, then return them home. The characters might be residents of the Lands of the Mists, making escape meaningless, Or escape from Ravenloft might motivate an entire campaign. If you make escape a focus of your campaign, consider the following questions: Possibility. Is it possible to escape the Domains of Dread against the will of the Dark Powers? Experts. Who might have knowledge regarding the nature of Ravenloft? What might the characters do to convince them to share their knowledge? Methods. What methods might allow the characters to escape? Is powerful magic enough, or does the nature of Ravenloft need to be altered? What costs might such methods have? Ramifications. will the characters’ escape only affect them, or will it have ramifications across the Domains of Dread (or the entire multiverse)? Will, Do the Dark Powers have a role in the characters’ escape? Must characters win these mysterious entities’ favor or find ways to defy them? Roll on or choose a result from Escape Adventure Seeds table to inspire adventure or campaign plots involving escape from the Domains of Dread. Escape Adventure Seeds 146 To Escape, the Characters Must... 1 Activate the Rift Spanner, a device fueled by souls stolen by the night hag Styrix. 2 Free Cthulhu, allowing it to escape into the multiverse. 3 Find Azalin Rex and discover how he escaped Darkon. 4 Journey into the past and prevent Strahd von Zarovich from becoming the first Darklord. 5 Sever a Darklord's tie to their domain, freeing the land back into the multiverse. 6 Use the device called the Apparatus to transport their souls into other bodies.

Darklord Ivan Dilisnya invites the objects of his obsessions to share in bizarre diversions at his estate, Deqravo # Maihope ENEMIES AND ALLIES This section presents individuals and groups who might appear anywhere in the Lands of the Mists, Such individuals and organizations might create continuity between domains, facilitate travel through the Mists, or provide support to characters. Alternatively, they might serve as opponents capable of confounding characters in multiple domains Mist WANDERERS Individuals who travel the Mists might ally with characters or share mysteries that lead to adventures elsewhere in the Domains of Dread. ALANIK Ray The Great Detective Alanik Ray is a peerless, elf investigator renowned in multiple domains as a master sleuth and solver of confounding mysteries. Alanik resides in Port-a-Lucine in Dementlieu with his husband, Arthur Sedgwick, but he investigates mysteries and aids sleuths across the Domains of Dread. ARTHUR SEDGWICK Pragmatic Investigator Together with his husband, Alanik Ray, the physician Arthur Sedgwick investigates criminal cases and paranormal mysteries. The practical half of the detective duo, Arthur chronicles their adventures, having published two volumes: The Life of Alanik Ray and The Casebook of Alanik Ray. Ez p'AVENIR Fearless Adventurer As a former pupil of Dr. Rudolph van Richten, Ez d’Avenir (detailed in chapter 5) is no stranger to the dangers haunting the Mists. Since venturing out on her own, Ez has gained a reputation as a skilled monster hunter and foe of evil. She often allies herself with other adventurers to put an end to evils. FIRAN ZALHONAN Scholar of Sinister Secrets

Page 211

The arrogant scholar Firan Zalhonan seeks the secrets underpinning the Domains of Dread. He plots to use his discoveries to escape the Mists into the "true realities” beyond, Firan hires agents to aid in his expeditions or to ferry him through the Mists—which he claims want to disrupt his efforts. Secretly Firan has ties to Azalin Rex, the missing Darklord of Darkon, or might be the Darklord in disguise (see chapter 2 for details). LarissA SNOWMANE Domain-Traveling Riverboat Captain Larissa Snowmane ferries travelers between the Domains of Dread aboard her riverboat, River Dancer. Larissa protects those traveling with her. In the most dire circumstance, she uses her mysterious magic, the Dance of the Dead, which can control the dead at the risk of slaying the living, Larissa learned her magic in part from Anton Misroi, Darklord of Souragne. She avoids that swampy domain at all costs. Larissa Snowmane's ship, River Dancer, navigates the waterways of the Domains of Dread # Sylvain Sarrailh Sava SUNSTAR Dhampir Vampire Hunter Savra Sunstar is a monster hunter and the dhampir daughter of Jander Sunstar, a vampire hero who saved Savra's mother from an early demise. For her part, Savra despises her father and seeks to slay him. She has devoted her life to hunting vampires and allies herself with like-minded adventurers. WEATHERMAY-FOXGROVE TWINS Paranormal Investigators The twins Gennifer and Laurie Weathermay-Foxgrove (detailed in chapter 5) are tenacious adventurers who follow their family's tradition of slaying monsters and defending the innocent. They reside in Mordent and have learned much from their surrogate uncle, Rudolph van Richten. ORGANIZATIONS These organizations span multiple domains. CHURCH OF EZRA Goal: Seek mercy from an ambivalent god Adventure Themes: Apathetic congregations, superstitions, witch hunting Pious souls in multiple domains pray to Ezra, an aloof god who personifies the Mists. Ezra's worshippers appear in congregations with varied (and sometimes conflicting) practices. The church serves largely as a formalization of local superstitions, whether in modest rural temples or urban cathedrals. When common folk give voice to their hopes or seek to ward off evil, they often pray to Ezra, THE CIRCLE Goal: Vanquish evil through acts of self-sacrifice Adventure Themes: Bravery, hubris, proof of heroism, quests against evil, tragedy Bold knights of the Circle quest from the Shadowlands in search of evil. More often than not, though, they find tragic fates. Even successful knights often venture back to the Shadowlands with evidence of their victories, potentially carrying evil back to their homeland. The knights of the Circle provide bold but reckless assistance to other would-be heroes, endlessly seeking to prove their valor.

Page 212

KEEPERS OF THE FEATHER Goal: Discover the paranormal and dispel evil Adventure Themes: Paranormal investigations, occult mysteries, mysticism The founding members of the Keepers of the Feather are wereravens (see chapter 5) in Barovia who oppose Strahd and whose reach extends to other domains. These Keepers are cautious, hiding their nature and guiding others so as not to jeopardize themselves or the mystical secrets they've gathered over generations. Many aided by these wereravens aren't ever aware of their assistance. In other realms, mysteries and talismans of the Keepers of the Feather have fallen into the hands of socialites and spiritualists. In social clubs, curious occultists pursue the mysteries of séances, fortune telling, metaphysical theories, and tales of the macabre. Such dabbling is as likely to cause catastrophes as it is to help those in need. These Keepers identify themselves with amulets that mimic the shape of a Barovian relic known as the Holy Symbol of Ravenkind. Both factions maintain a network of trained ravens that send messages between group members in different domains. ORDER OF THE GUARDIANS Goal: Destroy or contain cursed items and phenomena Adventure Themes: Ending curses, exorcisms, supernatural investigations, vault defense A network of monastic occultists, the Order of the Guardians hunts and destroys dangerous supernatural objects, cursed items, and stranger anomalies. if the Guardians can't destroy dangerous relics, they contain them within hidden, heavily warded vault-monasteries. The Guardians might share goals with characters trying to prevent supernatural dangers, but they might also consider characters who employ dangerous magic to be threats. PRIESTS OF OsYBUS Goal: Release Strahd von Zarovich Adventure Themes: Cult rituals, necromancy, secrets of the Domains of Dread The Priests of Osybus follow the teachings of a depraved lich and steal souls to transcend death. With their foul immortality, they seek to free Strahd von Zarovich from the Mists, an act they believe will cause the dissolution of the Domains of Dread. This inspires the priests to learn all they can about the deepest mysteries of the Mists. In shadowy cults, they conduct rituals aimed at escaping the Mists. These priests are detailed further in chapter 5. ULMIST INQUISITION Goal: Destroy the evil that dwells in wicked hearts Adventure Themes: Ends justifying the means, hunting evil, psionic interrogation The three branches of the Ulmist Inquisition employ psionic powers to stamp out evil, but their zeal and willingness to peer into others’ minds cause people to fear the inquisitors as much as the villains they oppose. Cells of the Ulmist Inquisition often ally with the Church of Ezra and oppose the priests of Osybus. Ulmist inquisitors might side with adventurers against evil, but they often test the virtue of their allies’ souls. These inquisitors are detailed further in chapter 5. VISTANI Goal: Find a way home, preserve age-old traditions, predict and avoid dangers in the Mists Adventure Themes: Reliable guides, stories from various lands, travel between domains Known throughout the Land of the Mists, Vistani (singular: Vistana) have a unique understanding of the Domains of Dread and the hidden paths between them. Following itinerant traditions, many Vistani and their adopted family members travel between domains, learning of distant lands, the many faces of evil, and strange wonders. Vistani never linger in one domain for long, as they endlessly seek a path to their legendary homeland. Horror ATMOSPHERE

Page 213

Light, music, and props all contribute to a frightful atmosphere # Alejandro Pacheco Frightful D&D adventures benefit from a game atmosphere that reinforces the horrifying plot. Consider these elements to influence your game's atmosphere, customizing what you can and not fretting about what you can't: Location, Strive to play in an area with minimal distractions that all players are comfortable in. Reduce distracting stimuli by turning off unnecessary screens and removing nongame materials, Lighting. Dimming the lights can cause distractions to fade and create an eerie ambiance in your game space. You can also adjust the lighting to reflect a setting’s light level, if i's easy for you to do so. However, always ensure the players have enough light to read by. Music, You can use music to reinforce an adventure's mood. Create playlists in advance, choosing options without lyrics and that aren't recognizably tied to popular media, If you can subtly control volume, slowly raise it when you want attention and lower it when players are talking, Experiment with how volume and tempo influence the mood of a scene. Props. Props and handouts are useful but shouldn't become chores or distractions. They might be as simple as a whiteboard for notes or a handwritten letter from a Darklord, Ravenloft has a history of featuring props in its adventures, such as the tarokka deck (see appendix A). HorROR ROLEPLAYING Consider the following methods to develop a sense of dread during your horror adventurers, DESCRIBING HORROR Rather than just naming a threat, inspire your players’ imaginations by describing the horror indirectly using the following techniques: Before It Arrives, Foreshadow the horror's approach. How do the environment, nearby animals, the weather, or shadows react to it?

Page 214

Approaches. What sounds does the horror make as it moves? What parts do characters see before the whole comes into view? Up Close. What does the horror smell like? How does it breathe? Does the air around it grow cold? Attacks. How does the horror attack? What does damage from it feel like? When it grabs a character, what does it feel like? Does it have physical weapons, or is it a supernatural force that warps other life? Wounded. When the horror is wounded, how does it react? What does its blood look like? What is inside it? HORROR PACING Not everything can be scary, and continuous terror is unsustainable. Before players grow numb to endless tension, create opportunities for them to catch their breath and regain a feeling of calm. The best horror adventures are like roller coasters, ratcheting up suspense and releasing it before building toward the next harrowing moment. IDLE UNEASINESS Occasionally slow your adventure's pace to draw out tension. Linger on describing details. Ask players to describe what their characters are thinking or feeling, or ask what the players think is the worst possible thing that could happen right now. Describe feelings of being watched. Let characters respond to these stray thoughts. Sometimes these impressions should just be their nervousness getting to them. But sometimes it's the calm before a threat strikes. Let PLAYERS FILL Gaps Invite players to turn their imaginations against themselves. Ask players to fill n details with questions like these: What does the way it moves. remind you of? It emits the worst smell—what is it? Something on the shelf reminds you of a nightmare—what is it? Players’ responses don't need to control the narrative; it's up to you whether these responses are true or just the characters’ perceptions. Moop MODELING Model the behaviors you want players to emulate by focusing on the game, letting others speak in turn, heading off encroachment on boundaries, and complimenting exciting choices, Stand or pace during intense moments. Speak faster or anxiously when something scary is happening. Raise your voice during pitched battles, and whisper during tense or conspiratorial moments. Allow yourself to sound uncertain, correct yourself, or pause as if you heard something outside. Ask players, “Are you sure?" By engaging with the suspenseful atmosphere you want to create, you bring it into being at the table. Out OF TIME When the time for action comes, make your narration urgent. Speak quickly and in short bursts. Focus less on senses and more on descriptions— a splash of crimson, who knows where it came from? Politely demand players react quickly: "There's no time to debate-what do you do?" RELIABILITY AND TRUST Reliability and trust are just as important to your horror adventures as lurid shocks. Frequent betrayals can make players hesitant, so emphasize that certain people and locations are trustworthy through repeated positive encounters. END ON CLIFFHANGERS Leave players excited to play again, When ending a session, implement one of the following to conclude on a cliffhanger: Hint at a new threat, impose a complication on existing plans, increase the stakes, introduce a new mystery, have a foe appear, have the world change, or otherwise shock the characters. Then call for a break before players can react. HAUNTED BASTIONS The rules for Bastions in the Dungeon Master's Guide allow characters to establish sanctuaries to rest and accrue resources between adventures, but these redoubts aren't outside the grasp of the Mists. BASTIONS IN THE MISTS Bastions in the Domains of Dread work well in campaigns that focus on a single domain or where Darklords aren't likely to seal their domains’ borders and cut characters off from their Bastions.

Page 215

Characters can create Bastions in any domain. The domains of Borca, Darkon, Lamordia, Mordent, and the Shadowlands are well suited to hosting Bastions due to their large size or aloof Darklords. Consider having characters inherit one or more existing structures—such as a ruined fortress, secluded tower, or house recently scoured of ghosts. Should characters adopt such a structure, they can refurbish it to their specifications. How Darklords respond to Bastions is up to you, but don't create plots that are hostile to Bastions. Darklords might ignore a Bastion, be curious about it, or use it as one more way to bind characters within their domains. The Dark Powers might intrude on any sanctuary, even characters’ personal Bastions # Ere! Maatita HAUNTED BASTIONS Haunted Bastions are afflicted by unusual supernatural events, These might be unpredictable annoyances at first, but clever characters might turn hauntings to their advantage. if you use this system, the first special facility characters create in their Bastion is Haunted. A Haunted special facility has the following rules: Irreplaceable. A Haunted special facility can't be replaced until it is no longer Haunted Repeating. While there is at least one Haunted special facility in a Bastion, whenever a character gains a new special facility or replaces a special facility with a different one, roll any die; on an even result, that new facility is Haunted When a character issues an order to a Haunted special facility or a Maintain order to their whole Bastion, roll 1d6; on a 1, roll on the Bastion Hauntings table and refer to the "Haunting Descriptions” section to see what happens. One of your Bastion's hirelings is cursed. As long as the hireling remains cursed, it won't leave the special facility it manages, and any work conducted in that facility takes twice as long to complete. Free-Roaming Spirit. A random special facility in your Bastion becomes Haunted. If all the special facilities in the Bastion are already Haunted, the DM rolls again on the Bastion Hauntings table. Friendly Ghost. A benevolent spirit possesses a hireling of the DM's choice. If the special facility where the hireling works produces items or Gold Pieces, it produces twice as much as usual as long as the hireling remains possessed, The spirit departs after 14 days. Hidden Room. The spirits reveal a hidden closet within your Bastion. Add a 5-foot-square space to your Bastion, Something might lie inside, at the DM's discretion. Lightning Storm. Trade orders issued this turn have no effect (including one that triggered this haunting, if applicable). If you have a Lightning Generator special facility, this Bastion Haunting has no effect. Living Dead. A group of mindless undead shamble from the Mists. The Attack Bastion event occurs, but roll 3d6 for that event rather than 6d6, Mist Wanderers. The Mists rise and 1d4 strangers emerge, seeking shelter. If there is room in your Bastion, they join as hirelings, Otherwise, they linger for 1d4 days before vanishing into the Mists. Mysterious Priest. A hooded priest appears at the Bastion, attracted by its haunting. Roll any die. One of the following occurs based on whether you rolled an even or an odd number on the die Inquisitor (Even). The priest is a witch hunter. If you have an Ancient Altar, Arcane Study, Cabinet of Curiosities, or Séance Parlor in your Bastion, the priest destroys it and you must replace it with a different special facility. If your Bastion has none of these facilities, the Extraordinary Opportunity Bastion event occurs. Exorcist (Odd). The priest is an exorcist. If you have a Reliquary, Sacristy, Sanctuary, or Sanctum in your Bastion, the priest offers to remove a haunting from one facility. If you agree, choose one Haunted facility; that facility is no longer Haunted. 4M Luck. Accidents and bad luck plague your Bastion. Harvest orders issued this turn have no effect (including one that triggered this haunting, if applicable). Poltergeist. Disruptive happenings plague the Bastion. Empower and Research orders issued this turn have no effect (including one that triggered this haunting, if applicable). Spatial Warp. The nature of a Bastion special facility changes. Roll 1d4, On a 4, replace one of your Bastior's special facilities with another for which you qualify; otherwise, increase the size of a Cramped special facility in your Bastion to Roomy or a Roomy one to Vast, at no cost. ‘Spectral Vision. A ghostly vision of a past event plays out. You gain some insight into the spirits that haunt your Bastion or the history of the surrounding land, as though affected by the Legend Lore spell Vistani Visitors. Vistani travelers visit your Bastion, drawn by tales of the spirits that inhabit it In return for a night's shelter, they offer to reveal your fate via a casting of the Augury spell or through a tarokka reading (see appendix A). If you turn them away, they depart, but the highest-level special facility in your Bastion that isnt already Haunted becomes Haunted. CHAPTER 4: CREATING DOMAINS OF DREAD Ahexblood witch in the domain of Tepest conjures visions of adventurers’ fatal fates (7 S200 A. Murray Any fear might find terrible life amid the Domains of Dread. Each domain highlights its own distinctive terror or flavor of the macabre. Chapter 2 presents numerous Domains of Dread and their respective Darklords, but you can blend legendary evils, unexpected monstrosities, and grim settings into your own horrifying creations. This chapter explores how to create a Domain of Dread, starting with defining its Darklord, the villain at the domain's heart. Descriptions of various genres of horror also provide details to guide and inspire your creations. TAROKKA AND RANDOM TABLES This section provides random tables compatible with the tarokka deck (see appendix A), Here's how to use the tarokka deck—or playing cards—instead of a d10 to generate random results: 1: Remove the High Deck. Remove all high deck cards, leaving only cards numbered one through nine plus the master of the suit. 2: Draw a Card. Ensure the deck is well shuffled, then draw from the top. 3: Consult a Table. Reference the card's number to find your result on a table; in the tarokka deck, the master of a suit counts as a ten. When using the tarokka deck, you can let the card's suit add extra dimension to your results. The four of coins might encourage you to link that aspect of the Darklord to wealth, while the four of swords could provide an interpretation rooted in violence. Appendix A details interpretations of the tarokka deck’s cards.

Page 219

CREATING A DARKLORD Domains are reflections of and prisons for the Darklords they confine. To devise a Domain of Dread, first consider its central antagonist and prisoner: its Darklord. The following sections detail how to develop a Darklord as a focal antagonist in your horror adventures. BUILDING YouR DARKLORD A Darklord's memories, desires, mistakes, and evil deeds shape a domain and its inhabitants. This chapter presents tables with suggested questions and motifs related to a Darklord's past and personality. Choose or randomly generate prompts to develop your Darklord further, or use the table entries as inspiration. To build a Darklord, follow these steps: Step 1: Choose Genres, What horror genres does your Darklord fit into? Use those genres’ principles to help develop your Darklord. Step 2: Determine Their Past. Who was the Darklord before they turned to irredeemable evil? What ambitions and flaws drove them to commit heinous acts? How did the Darklord first attract the attention of the Dark Powers? The ‘Darklord's Past" section provides a multitude of prompts to inspire your Darklord's origin story, Step 3: Determine Their Identity. What monstrous form does your Darklord's evil take? Turn to each genre chosen in step 1, then consult that genre's respective ‘Villain Identities" and "Villain Torments® sections. Feel free to mix and match the tables from different genres to achieve your desired feel. GENRES OF HORROR Horror stories span a wide variety of genres. One tale might explore the grotesque, warping flesh and bone into perversions of the living; another might be fantastical and philosophical, using restless ghosts to examine the nature of the soul. Before you begin building a Darklord, consider which genres of horror you'd like to explore. The Genres of Horror table describes the six major genres of horror explored later in this chapter and their common motifs. If you're uncertain where to start, use the table to identify which motifs interest, excite, or inspire fear in you. The sections later in the chapter also detail each genre's core principles, which drive how you develop the Darklord, the domain, and the adventures within. These genres aren't mutually exclusive, Feel free to blend themes from different genres to create a unique and terrifying story. The end of this chapter briefly describes a handful of additional genres for consideration. INTERVENTION OF THE DARK POWERS Consider how the Darklord attracted the attention of the Dark Powers. On the completion of an act of remarkable depravity, the Mists claim the Darklord—and perhaps their home—imprisoning them in a new Domain of Dread. The deed that ultimately causes a villain to be claimed by the Mists needn't be devastating, but it should represent an evil novel enough for the Dark Powers to take note. Use the Darklords' tales from chapter 2 to inspire how a Darklord might be claimed by the Mists. BIRTH OF A DARKLORD. Now trapped in a domain tailor-made to be their prison, the Darklord is immortal, and their greatest desire is just out of reach. At this point, you should have an idea of who the Darklord is, what they did, what form their evil takes, and how they came to the attention of the Dark Powers Now consider how the Darklord's evil manifests in their domain and how it brings them into conflict with the adventurers and the domain's inhabitants, MONSTROUS IDENTITIES Some Darklords undergo harrowing transformations on their imprisonment, as the Dark Powers warp those Darklords' forms to reflect their evil. Alternatively, the Darklord might have been a monster to begin with, or pethaps they gained their form and powers through their deeds or a curse Consider how your Darklord is viewed by inhabitants of their domain. Reference the genres of horror that your Darklord fits into (later in this chapter), and use a genre's ‘Villain Identities" and "Villain Torments" sections to shape the Darklord’s monstrousness. The ‘Villain Identities" sections provide options for the Darklord's primary identity or form while within their domain, while the "Villain Torments" sections provide torturous twists that the Dark Powers might employ to torment the Darklord and reflect their evil

Page 221

FINISHING TOUCHES The story of a Darklord entwines with those of the adventurers and the domain itself. As you finish fleshing out your Darklord’s details, consider the following options to further connect the Darklord to their domain and the adventures within it. Endless Torment, In every domain, its Darklord's greatest desire remains in view but ever out of reach. How does your Darklord's torment remain central to and unignorable in the story? Misty Borders. The Mists of Ravenloft surround each Domain of Dread. The Dark Powers afford Darklords some control over the Mists bordering their domains. Does your Darklord allow the Mists to function undisturbed, or do the Mists also reflect the Darklord’s nature, perpetuating the cycle of torment? Supporting Cast. Consider what NPC types support the Darklord, exacerbate their threat, or oppose them. How do the inhabitants of the domain play into the Dark Powers’ plans, taking the Darklord's situation from bad to worse? CREATING A DOMAIN Domains of Dread might be any size or shape and can draw inspiration from any horror genre # Chris Seaman Once you've developed a terrifying Darklord, start shaping that Darklord’s unique Domain of Dread, Everything in this realm is inspired by the Darklord’s torment or personalized to it. This section provides guidelines to help you create a suitably terrifying and tragic Domain of Dread, BUILDING A DOMAIN Similar to building a Darklord, you can choose or randomly generate prompts from the tables below to flesh out the domain's details. To build a Domain of Dread, follow these steps: Step 1: Choose Genres, Most Domains of Dread are inspired by the same horror genres that shape their imprisoned Darklord, but feel free to mix in new genre elements. ‘Step 2: Develop Details. What are the distinctive elements of the domain? How do these elements reflect and twist the Darklord’s past, furthering the Darklord's torment? The "Dread Details” section contains several tables with prompts to tailor your domain to its central prisoner. ‘Step 3: Create Adventures. What adventures will the characters have in this domain? Turn to the genres chosen in step 1, and consult their "Adventure Hooks" and “Adventure Settings" sections to inspire your adventures. Step 4: Choose Monsters. The genres later in this chapter each include a table suggesting a plethora of monsters that suit the genre's sensibilities. Consult those monster tables, and choose which terrifying creatures will haunt the characters in the domain. If none of the suggested monsters fit your ideas, you can reskin or alter the suggested monsters or choose ones that do.

Dreab DETAILS The domain's buildings, natural elements, landmarks, and creatures reflect the Darklord's vile past. Things like weather, ecosystems, and agriculture needn't follow logic in a domain, Mundane details can fade into the background as your adventures focus on the domain's threats. DoMAIN ELEMENTS Above all else, a domain is a torturous prison for its Darklord. Visceral sights, sounds, and smells within the domain should hint at the Darklord's past villainy or serve as a means for the Dark Powers to torment the domain's central prisoner. UNLEASH YOUR HORROR At this point, you should have a wealth of information about your Darklord and their domain. Review your notes, connect elements, establish recurring themes, and consider ways to foreshadow circumstances that adventures might focus on. You don't need to have answers for every question now; you can define many details during adventures or by focusing on elements your players gravitate toward. For examples of fully detailed Darklords and domains, see chapter 2. GENRES OF HORROR

Page 223

The following sections provide overviews of several genres of horror. Use these to inspire adventures, Darklords, and Domains of Dread of your own creation or to further detail the domains presented in chapter 2. Mix and match elements from different genres to create your own uniquely terrifying combinations. Bopy Horror # Wan Shavrin Body horror examines the fear of our failing anatomies. Tales in this genre explore the unpredictability of flesh—that robust health might be an illusion and that we might be incubating what will devour us. Examples include Alien and The Fly. Bopy Horror PRINCIPLES Consider the following principles when creating Darklords and domains with body horror themes: Depth of Transformation. Physical transformations are common and might affect more than bodies. Objects, architecture, and the natural world might be also anthropomorphized. Flesh Aberrations. Characters suffer aberrant or excessive growth, whether of organs, discordant appendages, or unnatural materials. Gore Is a Byproduct. Though gore might take center stage in body horror stories, it's often a side effect of stories about fear of change, difference, mortality, physical harm, or disease. Gore Isn't Required. Body horror doesn't need to be messy. Swapping bodies, turning to stone, or gaining one’s ideal form might feature in this genre. Real Disabilities Aren't Horrifying. Body horror has a history of portraying disability as monstrous. Avoid this trope.

Page 224

Bopy Horror VILLAINS A physical transformation doesn't make someone a villain. Villains in body horror adventures cultivate evil in tandem with the aberrant transformations afflicting their form and surroundings. VILLAIN IDENTITIES Villains of this genre are often tragic figures, whether they're cultists enduring forced metamorphosis or scientists trying to extend their lives. Choose from or roll on the Body Horror Villains table to inspire villains appropriate to this genre. Cosmic Horror # Wan Shavrin Cosmic horror revolves around the fear of personal insignificance. The genre, inspired by stories of the Cthulhu Mythos, is predicated on the idea of entities so vast and so genuinely beyond our comprehension that we car't fathom their simplest motivations. To see them is to become lost in their magnitude and the evidence that we have never and could never matter to the cosmos at large. Examples include Annihilation and Stalker. Cosmic HORROR PRINCIPLES Consider the following principles when creating Darklords and domains with cosmic horror themes: Fear the Unknown. Be vague. Cosmic horror emerges through imagination and the indescribable, not through details. Iconography. Cults, forbidden books, and strange symbols form the cornerstones of cosmic horror. Inscrutable Forces. Cosmic horror is about alien forces driven by motivations humans can't understand. There is no good or evil, no law or chaos, Marginalization Isn't Horror. The genre has a history of framing marginalized demographics as monstrous and stigmatizing mental illness. Avoid those tropes. Remove the Familiar. Cosmic horror makes characters feel gradually unmoored from reality Cosmic HorROR VILLAINS Despite the genre's grand magnitude, villains of cosmic horror are still often individual beings. Godlike terrors and inscrutable forces might pull the strings with their unknowable goals, but the antagonists the characters face are concrete, fallible people.

Page 227

VILLAIN IDENTITIES Cosmic horror villains often perpetrate horrors as they strive for outcomes they can't articulate. Choose from or roll on the Cosmic Horror Villains. table to inspire villains appropriate to this genre. Cosmic Horror Villains 1410 The Villain Is... 1 Assmiling minstrel whose music drives listeners to murder. A priest obsessed with creating a shelter safe from the coming apocalypse. Ascientist striving to make their body the perfect host for an ageless being. ‘A coroner who believes the bodies they autopsy contain secret messages. The head librarian of an ancient sect, who seeks secrets hidden within their peers. A spoiled noble who intends to raise a cult to feed to residents of an alien dimension. Cor annwn A researcher knowingly incubating a powerful entity within themself. 3 Ahierophant seeking a doorway to an alien realm to bring about a new world. Cosmic HORROR ADVENTURES ‘Adventures in cosmic horror realms are bleak, desperate affairs, where the best one can hope for is to survive relatively intact. There is no stopping the ultimate evil, but characters might aspire to seal it away temporarily. ADVENTURE HOOKS Cosmic horror adventures often deal with how alien forces might alter us, perverting our expectations and separating us from what makes us human. Choose from or roll on the Cosmic Horror Plots table to inspire your cosmic horror adventures, Cosmic Horror Plots 1410 ‘Adventure Hook 1 A child has gone missing in the impossibly vast space beneath their bed, Something drags a coastal town's inhabitants out in the night, garbs them in silver, and leads them to the surf to drown. DARK FANTASY Wan Shavrin The dark fantasy genre features fantasy worlds where grim themes, nihilistic plots, or horrifying elements inform their stories. Any tale featuring both supernatural elements and horror themes might constitute dark fantasy, whether it's a fantasy story steeped in dread or an otherwise realistic world that features a supernatural terror. Examples include The Dark Tower and Pan's Labyrinth, DarK FANTASY PRINCIPLES Consider the following principles when creating Darklords and domains with dark fantasy themes: Evil Sometimes Triumphs. Good doesn't always win. Evil individuals with great power and unopposed schemes might prosper. Low Magic. Magic and magic items might be rarer or require a bargain or sacrifice to use. Morally Gray. The lines between good and evil are blurred. Choices involve deciding on the least bad outcome available. Nefarious Deeds, Corruption and suspicion flourish among organizations and individuals. Reluctant Heroes. Antiheroes are common—characters tainted by the world’s evil or those who refuse to be considered heroes. Dark FANTASY VILLAINS Sinister individuals leave their marks on dark fantasy domains, whether with oppressive architecture, unavoidable propaganda, or scars on an exploited environment, VILLAIN IDENTITIES Villains occupy places of influence in dark fantasy domains. Choose from or roll on the Dark Fantasy Villains table to inspire villains appropriate to this genre.

FOLK Horror # Wan Shavrin Folk horror adventures involve traditions, beliefs, and perceptions that are passed down through generations and take terrifying twists. For those who ascribe to hidden traditions, sacrifices to strange gods or placations to lurking monsters are everyday events. For outsiders, though, these practices reveal the subjectivity of normalcy and societal truth. Examples include Midsommar and The Wicker Man. FOLK Horror PRINCIPLES Consider the following principles when creating Darklords and domains with folk horror themes: Isolation Begets Weirdness. Strange and potentially dangerous traditions flourish in isolated or private communities. Perversion of Community. Communities in folk horror stories often are a grim mirror of an aspect of accepted society. They often dramatically use outsiders or purge nonbelievers. Relative Truth. Beliefs highlighted in folk horror stories might not be true Secret Traditions. Community members typically hide their beliefs, perhaps by physically obscuring them or manipulating others in power. Strangeness in Specificity. Art, symbols, tools, celebrations, and other trappings of belief make a community's traditions more specific and eerie. Fok Horror VILLAINS Antagonistic forces in folk horror usually are community leaders—paragons of the strange beliefs central to the horror's narrative. Outsiders might have a chance to adopt the community's ways, but otherwise locals consider them heretics or corrupting elements.

Page 233

Guost STORIES # Wan Shavrin Ghost stories number among the more psychologically elaborate genres of horror, Only by revealing tales of tragedy and past wrongs can heroes bring peace to forces that share their suffering with the living. Examples include The Woman in Black and The Haunting of Hill House. GuosT STorY PRINCIPLES Consider the following principles when creating Darklords and domains inspired by ghost stories: Mirror of Humanity. Ghosts are a way to examine the nature of the soul, mortality, and history. Nalve Heroes. Heroes are purehearted or unsuspecting individuals whose resolve is shaken by the story's events. Look for ways to test heroes’ psychology with your hauntings. Resolving a Haunt. Heroes need a way to put spirits to rest. Once you reveal the story, ensure the way to combat the haunting is clear. Specificity, Even the smallest details can reveal a haunting's story. A simple locket or portrait might contain clues that explain a haunting, Ties to the Living, Personal ties give ghost stories weight. Consider connecting the heroes to spirits in ways they won't predict, such as making a phantasmal villain a hero's ancestor. Guost Story VILLAINS In ghost stories, spirits often represent instruments of supernatural justice, causing those responsible for their deaths to suffer. Spirits also can represent grief and the need for closure,

Page 236

Gortuic Horror Wan Shavrin Gothic horror deals with the terror within, it shatters the illusion of humanity by holding a mirror up to us and forcing us to examine what we truly are and what we pretend to be, In gothic horror, we—not some distant entity—are the architects of our destruction. Examples include Dracula and Frankenstein. Goruic HorROR PRINCIPLES Consider the following principles when creating Darklords and domains with gothic horror themes: Brave Heroes. Gothic heroes are often virtuous, deeply passionate, or courageous. Adventures should test characters’ beliefs and morality. Foreboding. Atmosphere and a sense of dread are key. Locations are often decrepit or foreboding. High Emotions. Gothic stories include intense, exaggerated emotions. Romances, rivalries, and life-changing events are common. Sacrifice. Sacrifices feature prominently in gothic stories. Give characters heart-wrenching choices. Unassuming, Unrepentant Villains. Gothic villains are unrepentantly evil, but this shouldn't be immediately obvious. Drop hints about your villain's awful secrets before revealing them fully. Goruic Horror VILLAINS Though thoroughly wicked, villains in gothic horror tales act reserved or unassuming until they reveal their true nature. Any intriguing figure with a dark secret can serve as a gothic horror villain.

Page 239

VILLAIN IDENTITIES Gothic villains are most effective when they are slowly revealed as shockingly cruel, immoral, or the antithesis of good.
